It feels like it takes too much energy just to say anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with not wanting to do anything and having low energy?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been since I started that community-college program. So… like two years ago? Actually a little more—two years and maybe two months. It kind of all started slipping around then, though I didn’t notice it right away because I was trying to keep track of classes and the bus schedule, and there was this vending machine near the hallway that always took my coins but… yeah. Since then, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, like activities you did before such as knitting, journaling, or walking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy them now. I mean, knitting used to feel… kind of calming, like counting the stitches, and I had this blue yarn that got all tangled in a drawer, but now I just look at it and don’t do anything. Same with journaling. I’ll think maybe I should write something, and then I just sit there.\n\nWalking too—I used to go out more, even just around the block, but now it’s like I can’t make myself leave my room. I’ll be in the same clothes and not even really notice. When my mom asks what’s wrong, I barely have the energy to answer her.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re not worth much lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t know. Not really like guilt, I guess? I mean, sometimes my mom says things and I can tell she’s tired, and then I start thinking about the dishes in the sink or how the light in the hallway flickers and nobody fixes it, but… I don’t know if that’s the same thing.\n\nWorth much… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mostly just feel kind of blank. Like I’m there, but not doing anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t know if I’d call it depressed. Like… I’m not crying all day or anything. It’s more like everything is kind of far away, or quiet, even when the TV is on.

I guess… slower, maybe. Not like I’m trying to be. I just sit in my room and it’s like I can’t get started on anything. I’ll still be wearing the same clothes, and my mom comes in and asks what’s wrong, but answering feels like… too much work.

I used to knit sometimes, and write in my journal, and I’d go walk around the block, especially when the sky looked kind of orange in the evening. There was this one house with little ceramic ducks by the steps, and I always wondered why they faced the street instead of the door… but I don’t really do that now. Mostly I just sit there. I don’t feel fidgety. It’s more like everything takes too much effort, even talking.

Yeah… I think so. Like, walking, I don’t know, but doing things feels slow because I just can’t make myself start. I’ll sit in my room in the same clothes and it’s like the whole day gets thick or something.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really feel… much interest, no. Like I look at the knitting bag and it’s just there. The yarn kind of spills out of it sometimes because the zipper catches, and I used to like choosing colors, but now it feels like something across the room that belongs to someone else.

Walking is the same. I used to go around the block, and there’s that house with the ducks by the steps—I think I told you that already—but I don’t go see them now. I mostly just sit in my room with the same clothes on, and then hours pass. My journal’s there too, but I don’t write.

It’s not exactly that I hate those things. I just can’t make myself do them, and if my mom asks what’s wrong, even answering her feels like too much energy.

  },

I don’t know… I haven’t really kept track. My mom cooks sometimes and there’ll be plates in the sink, and I’ll think about how the spoon always gets left in the bowl and then it dries like that, and it’s hard to wash after.

But appetite… I mean, I don’t think I noticed losing weight or anything. My clothes feel the same, I guess. I eat when food is there sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s less than before. I don’t really pay attention to it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if “depressed” is the right word. Like… I don’t wake up and think that word exactly. It’s more like the day is just sitting there, if that makes sense.

Sometimes the light comes through the blinds in stripes, and I’ll stare at that for a long time, and then I realize I was supposed to get up or do something. My mom says the house gets quiet when I’m like this, but houses make sounds anyway—like the fridge clicks and the pipes do that tapping thing.

So… most of the day? I’m not sure. I guess I don’t feel good, but I don’t know if it’s sad or down exactly. It’s more blank.

I just can’t make myself do them, and if my mom asks what’s wrong, even answering her feels like too much energy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than before or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really kept track. My mom cooks sometimes and there’ll be plates in the sink, and I’ll think about how the spoon always gets left in the bowl and then it dries like that, and it’s hard to wash after.\n\nBut appetite… I mean, I don’t think I noticed losing weight or anything. My clothes feel the same, I guess. I eat when food is there sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s less than before. I don’t really pay attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “depressed” is the right word. Like… I don’t wake up and think that word exactly. It’s more like the day is just sitting there, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes the light comes through the blinds in stripes, and I’ll stare at that for a long time, and then I realize I was supposed to get up or do something. My mom says the house gets quiet when I’m like this, but houses make sounds anyway—like the fridge clicks and the pipes do that tapping thing.\n\nSo… most of the day? I’m not sure. I guess I don’t feel good, but I don’t know if it’s sad or down exactly. It’s more blank.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you have completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing really holds your attention or feels meaningful anymore?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… completely, I don’t know. That sounds like a big word. But mostly, yeah, nothing really pulls me toward it anymore.

I just sit in my room a lot, in the same clothes, and the knitting bag is there and my journal is there, but I don’t pick them up. I used to walk too, like around the block, but now even thinking about shoes and the door and all that feels like too much.

And when my mom asks what’s wrong, I can hear her, but answering feels… heavy. Like I don’t have enough in me to even explain it.

I mostly just feel kind of blank. Like I’m there, but not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep or waking up too early, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I ran or did anything, I’m just… there.

Mostly I sit in my room, and I’ll still be in the same clothes, and the stuff is right there—my knitting bag, my journal—and I look at it but I don’t do it. I used to go walking, but now even that feels like there are too many steps before the actual walking, like finding socks and shoes and opening the door.

And my mom will ask what’s wrong, and I hear her, I do, but answering feels like trying to lift something heavy. Like I don’t even have enough energy to explain.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I just can’t make myself do anything anymore. I mostly sit in my room, and I’ll realize I’m still wearing the same clothes and I didn’t even think to change. I used to knit sometimes, and journal, and I’d go walking—there’s this one street near my house with really uneven sidewalks, I always noticed that—but now I just don’t do any of it.\n\nMy mom keeps asking me what’s wrong, and I don’t even know how to answer her. It feels like it takes too much energy just to say anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with not wanting to do anything and having low energy?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been since I started that community-college program. So… like two years ago? Actually a little more—two years and maybe two months. It kind of all started slipping around then, though I didn’t notice it right away because I was trying to keep track of classes and the bus schedule, and there was this vending machine near the hallway that always took my coins but… yeah. Since then, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, like activities you did before such as knitting, journaling, or walking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy them now. I mean, knitting used to feel… kind of calming, like counting the stitches, and I had this blue yarn that got all tangled in a drawer, but now I just look at it and don’t do anything. Same with journaling. I’ll think maybe I should write something, and then I just sit there.\n\nWalking too—I used to go out more, even just around the block, but now it’s like I can’t make myself leave my room. I’ll be in the same clothes and not even really notice. When my mom asks what’s wrong, I barely have the energy to answer her.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re not worth much lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t know. Not really like guilt, I guess? I mean, sometimes my mom says things and I can tell she’s tired, and then I start thinking about the dishes in the sink or how the light in the hallway flickers and nobody fixes it, but… I don’t know if that’s the same thing.\n\nWorth much… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mostly just feel kind of blank. Like I’m there, but not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep or waking up too early, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I don’t keep track of it very well. Sometimes I’m in bed for a long time, but I don’t know if I’m sleeping or just lying there, you know? The clock has those red numbers and they kind of make the room look strange at night, and then I start thinking about whether I left a cup on the floor.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed. Like… I’m not crying all day or anything. It’s more like everything is kind of far away, or quiet, even when the TV is on.\n\nSome days I look out the window and the same car is parked there, the red one with the dent, and I’ll just keep staring at it and then it’s like an hour passed. But I don’t know if that means I’m down. I just feel… not much, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or had thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know how to answer that. Life being worth living sounds like one of those things people say on posters at school, like in the counselor’s office, with the ocean picture behind it.\n\nI haven’t really… I mean, I don’t sit there planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I just kind of don’t think about it at all. Like I’ll be holding a spoon and then notice the pattern on it, and it’s like everything goes quiet for a while.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you move or speak more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, that you’re more restless or fidgety than before?\n\nPatient: I guess… slower, maybe. Not like I’m trying to be. I just sit in my room and it’s like I can’t get started on anything. I’ll still be wearing the same clothes, and my mom comes in and asks what’s wrong, but answering feels like… too much work.\n\nI used to knit sometimes, and write in my journal, and I’d go walk around the block, especially when the sky looked kind of orange in the evening. There was this one house with little ceramic ducks by the steps, and I always wondered why they faced the street instead of the door… but I don’t really do that now. Mostly I just sit there. I don’t feel fidgety. It’s more like everything takes too much effort, even talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements, like walking or doing simple tasks, have become slower than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like, walking, I don’t know, but doing things feels slow because I just can’t make myself start. I’ll sit in my room in the same clothes and it’s like the whole day gets thick or something.\n\nI used to knit, and I had this journal with a blue cover—there was a little rip near the corner because I kept it in my backpack with pens loose in there—but I don’t really pick it up now. And walking too, I used to go out sometimes, just around the block, but now I just… don’t.\n\nWhen my mom asks me what’s wrong, I barely answer. Not because I’m mad at her. It just takes too much energy to make the words come out.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you feel when you try to do things you used to enjoy, like knitting or walking—do you feel any interest or pleasure in them at all now?\n\nPatient: I don’t really feel… much interest, no. Like I look at the knitting bag and it’s just there. The yarn kind of spills out of it sometimes because the zipper catches, and I used to like choosing colors, but now it feels like something across the room that belongs to someone else.\n\nWalking is the same. I used to go around the block, and there’s that house with the ducks by the steps—I think I told you that already—but I don’t go see them now. I mostly just sit in my room with the same clothes on, and then hours pass. My journal’s there too, but I don’t write.\n\nIt’s not exactly that I hate those things. I just can’t make myself do them, and if my mom asks what’s wrong, even answering her feels like too much energy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than before or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really kept track. I don’t really pay attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “depressed” is the right word. Like… I don’t wake up and think that word exactly. It’s more like the day is just sitting there, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes the light comes through the blinds in stripes, and I’ll stare at that for a long time, and then I realize I was supposed to get up or do something. My mom says the house gets quiet when I’m like this, but houses make sounds anyway—like the fridge clicks and the pipes do that tapping thing.\n\nSo… most of the day? I’m not sure. I guess I don’t feel good, but I don’t know if it’s sad or down exactly. It’s more blank.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing really holds your attention or feels meaningful anymore?\n\nPatient: I mean… completely, I don’t know. I guess I don’t feel good, but I don’t know if it’s sad or down exactly. It’s more blank.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing really holds your attention or feels meaningful anymore?\n\nPatient: I mean… completely, I don’t know. Like I don’t have enough in me to even explain it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve felt a loss of energy or unexplained fatigue nearly every day, even when you haven’t done anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It feels like it takes too much energy just to say anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with not wanting to do anything and having low energy?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been since I started that community-college program. So… like two years ago? Actually a little more—two years and maybe two months. It kind of all started slipping around then, though I didn’t notice it right away because I was trying to keep track of classes and the bus schedule, and there was this vending machine near the hallway that always took my coins but… yeah. Since then, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, like activities you did before such as knitting, journaling, or walking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy them now. I mean, knitting used to feel… kind of calming, like counting the stitches, and I had this blue yarn that got all tangled in a drawer, but now I just look at it and don’t do anything. I mostly just feel kind of blank. Like I’m there, but not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep or waking up too early, or on the flip side, do you find yourself sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I don’t feel fidgety. It’s more like everything takes too much effort, even talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements, like walking or doing simple tasks, have become slower than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll sit in my room in the same clothes and it’s like the whole day gets thick or something.\n\nI used to knit, and I had this journal with a blue cover—there was a little rip near the corner because I kept it in my backpack with pens loose in there—but I don’t really pick it up now. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed. Like… I’m not crying all day or anything. It’s more like everything is kind of far away, or quiet, even when the TV is on.\n\nSome days I look out the window and the same car is parked there, the red one with the dent, and I’ll just keep staring at it and then it’s like an hour passed. But I don’t know if that means I’m down. I just feel… not much, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or had thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know how to answer that. Life being worth living sounds like one of those things people say on posters at school, like in the counselor’s office, with the ocean picture behind it.\n\nI haven’t really… I mean, I don’t sit there planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I just kind of don’t think about it at all. Like I’ll be holding a spoon and then notice the pattern on it, and it’s like everything goes quiet for a while.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you move or speak more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, that you’re more restless or fidgety than before?\n\nPatient: I guess… slower, maybe. Not like I’m trying to be. I just sit in my room and it’s like I can’t get started on anything. I’ll still be wearing the same clothes, and my mom comes in and asks what’s wrong, but answering feels like… too much work.\n\nI used to knit sometimes, and write in my journal, and I’d go walk around the block, especially when the sky looked kind of orange in the evening. There was this one house with little ceramic ducks by the steps, and I always wondered why they faced the street instead of the door… but I don’t really do that now. Mostly I just sit there. I don’t feel fidgety. It’s more like everything takes too much effort, even talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements, like walking or doing simple tasks, have become slower than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like, walking, I don’t know, but doing things feels slow because I just can’t make myself start. I’ll sit in my room in the same clothes and it’s like the whole day gets thick or something.\n\nI used to knit, and I had this journal with a blue cover—there was a little rip near the corner because I kept it in my backpack with pens loose in there—but I don’t really pick it up now. And walking too, I used to go out sometimes, just around the block, but now I just… don’t.\n\nWhen my mom asks me what’s wrong, I barely answer. Not because I’m mad at her. It just takes too much energy to make the words come out.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you feel when you try to do things you used to enjoy, like knitting or walking—do you feel any interest or pleasure in them at all now?\n\nPatient: I don’t really feel… much interest, no. Like I look at the knitting bag and it’s just there. The yarn kind of spills out of it sometimes because the zipper catches, and I used to like choosing colors, but now it feels like something across the room that belongs to someone else.\n\nWalking is the same. I used to go around the block, and there’s that house with the ducks by the steps—I think I told you that already—but I don’t go see them now. I mostly just sit in my room with the same clothes on, and then hours pass. My journal’s there too, but I don’t write.\n\nIt’s not exactly that I hate those things. I just can’t make myself do them, and if my mom asks what’s wrong, even answering her feels like too much energy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than before or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really kept track. My mom cooks sometimes and there’ll be plates in the sink, and I’ll think about how the spoon always gets left in the bowl and then it dries like that, and it’s hard to wash after.\n\nBut appetite… I mean, I don’t think I noticed losing weight or anything. My clothes feel the same, I guess. I eat when food is there sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s less than before. I don’t really pay attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “depressed” is the right word. Like… I don’t wake up and think that word exactly. It’s more like the day is just sitting there, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes the light comes through the blinds in stripes, and I’ll stare at that for a long time, and then I realize I was supposed to get up or do something. My mom says the house gets quiet when I’m like this, but houses make sounds anyway—like the fridge clicks and the pipes do that tapping thing.\n\nSo… most of the day? I’m not sure. I guess I don’t feel good, but I don’t know if it’s sad or down exactly. It’s more blank.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing really holds your attention or feels meaningful anymore?\n\nPatient: I mean… completely, I don’t know. That sounds like a big word. But mostly, yeah, nothing really pulls me toward it anymore.\n\nI just sit in my room a lot, in the same clothes, and the knitting bag is there and my journal is there, but I don’t pick them up. I used to walk too, like around the block, but now even thinking about shoes and the door and all that feels like too much.\n\nAnd when my mom asks what’s wrong, I can hear her, but answering feels… heavy. Like I don’t have enough in me to even explain it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve felt a loss of energy or unexplained fatigue nearly every day, even when you haven’t done anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I ran or did anything, I’m just… there.\n\nMostly I sit in my room, and I’ll still be in the same clothes, and the stuff is right there—my knitting bag, my journal—and I look at it but I don’t do it. I used to go walking, but now even that feels like there are too many steps before the actual walking, like finding socks and shoes and opening the door.\n\nAnd my mom will ask what’s wrong, and I hear her, I do, but answering feels like trying to lift something heavy. Like I don’t even have enough energy to explain.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for most of the past two years?\n\nPatient: Two years… I don’t know. That’s hard to measure. I don’t really keep it in like… blocks like that.\n\nI know there have been long stretches where things felt kind of gray, like the same day repeating, but then I start thinking about school being over and how the calendar in the kitchen still has an old month on it, and my mom keeps saying she’ll change it but she doesn’t. So time gets weird.\n\nMost of the day, nearly every day… I can’t say that exactly. I’m not sure. It’s been a while, but I don’t know if I can put two years on it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I still don’t know how to answer that exactly. Two years sounds so… measured, like I should have been marking it on a calendar or something.

The clock has those red numbers and they kind of make the room look strange at night, and then I start thinking about whether I left a cup on the floor.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed. Like… I’m not crying all day or anything. It’s more like everything is kind of far away, or quiet, even when the TV is on.\n\nSome days I look out the window and the same car is parked there, the red one with the dent, and I’ll just keep staring at it and then it’s like an hour passed. But I don’t know if that means I’m down. I just feel… not much, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or had thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know how to answer that. Life being worth living sounds like one of those things people say on posters at school, like in the counselor’s office, with the ocean picture behind it.\n\nI haven’t really… I mean, I don’t sit there planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I just kind of don’t think about it at all. Like I’ll be holding a spoon and then notice the pattern on it, and it’s like everything goes quiet for a while.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you move or speak more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, that you’re more restless or fidgety than before?\n\nPatient: I guess… slower, maybe. Not like I’m trying to be. I just sit in my room and it’s like I can’t get started on anything. I’ll still be wearing the same clothes, and my mom comes in and asks what’s wrong, but answering feels like… too much work.\n\nI used to knit sometimes, and write in my journal, and I’d go walk around the block, especially when the sky looked kind of orange in the evening. There was this one house with little ceramic ducks by the steps, and I always wondered why they faced the street instead of the door… but I don’t really do that now. Mostly I just sit there. I don’t feel fidgety. It’s more like everything takes too much effort, even talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements, like walking or doing simple tasks, have become slower than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like, walking, I don’t know, but doing things feels slow because I just can’t make myself start. I’ll sit in my room in the same clothes and it’s like the whole day gets thick or something.\n\nI used to knit, and I had this journal with a blue cover—there was a little rip near the corner because I kept it in my backpack with pens loose in there—but I don’t really pick it up now. And walking too, I used to go out sometimes, just around the block, but now I just… don’t.\n\nWhen my mom asks me what’s wrong, I barely answer. Not because I’m mad at her. It just takes too much energy to make the words come out.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you feel when you try to do things you used to enjoy, like knitting or walking—do you feel any interest or pleasure in them at all now?\n\nPatient: I don’t really feel… much interest, no. Like I look at the knitting bag and it’s just there. The yarn kind of spills out of it sometimes because the zipper catches, and I used to like choosing colors, but now it feels like something across the room that belongs to someone else.\n\nWalking is the same. I used to go around the block, and there’s that house with the ducks by the steps—I think I told you that already—but I don’t go see them now. I mostly just sit in my room with the same clothes on, and then hours pass. My journal’s there too, but I don’t write.\n\nIt’s not exactly that I hate those things. I just can’t make myself do them, and if my mom asks what’s wrong, even answering her feels like too much energy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than before or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really kept track. My mom cooks sometimes and there’ll be plates in the sink, and I’ll think about how the spoon always gets left in the bowl and then it dries like that, and it’s hard to wash after.\n\nBut appetite… I mean, I don’t think I noticed losing weight or anything. My clothes feel the same, I guess. I eat when food is there sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s less than before. I don’t really pay attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “depressed” is the right word. Like… I don’t wake up and think that word exactly. It’s more like the day is just sitting there, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes the light comes through the blinds in stripes, and I’ll stare at that for a long time, and then I realize I was supposed to get up or do something. My mom says the house gets quiet when I’m like this, but houses make sounds anyway—like the fridge clicks and the pipes do that tapping thing.\n\nSo… most of the day? I’m not sure. I guess I don’t feel good, but I don’t know if it’s sad or down exactly. It’s more blank.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing really holds your attention or feels meaningful anymore?\n\nPatient: I mean… completely, I don’t know. That sounds like a big word. But mostly, yeah, nothing really pulls me toward it anymore.\n\nI just sit in my room a lot, in the same clothes, and the knitting bag is there and my journal is there, but I don’t pick them up. I used to walk too, like around the block, but now even thinking about shoes and the door and all that feels like too much.\n\nAnd when my mom asks what’s wrong, I can hear her, but answering feels… heavy. Like I don’t have enough in me to even explain it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve felt a loss of energy or unexplained fatigue nearly every day, even when you haven’t done anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I ran or did anything, I’m just… there.\n\nMostly I sit in my room, and I’ll still be in the same clothes, and the stuff is right there—my knitting bag, my journal—and I look at it but I don’t do it. I used to go walking, but now even that feels like there are too many steps before the actual walking, like finding socks and shoes and opening the door.\n\nAnd my mom will ask what’s wrong, and I hear her, I do, but answering feels like trying to lift something heavy. Like I don’t even have enough energy to explain.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for most of the past two years?\n\nPatient: Two years… I don’t know. That’s hard to measure. I don’t really keep it in like… blocks like that.\n\nI know there have been long stretches where things felt kind of gray, like the same day repeating, but then I start thinking about school being over and how the calendar in the kitchen still has an old month on it, and my mom keeps saying she’ll change it but she doesn’t. So time gets weird.\n\nMost of the day, nearly every day… I can’t say that exactly. I’m not sure. It’s been a while, but I don’t know if I can put two years on it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two years?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know how to answer that exactly. Two years sounds so… measured, like I should have been marking it on a calendar or something.\n\nI know there’s been a lot of time where I feel low, or like everything is kind of muted, but then some days I can’t tell if it’s depressed or just tired or just the day being weird. And the past two years had so many little pieces in it—school ending, being home more, my mom asking things, the same room—so it all kind of blends together.\n\nSo… I can’t really say “yes, most of the day, nearly every day, for two years.” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that for the past two years, most days you felt down or depressed for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure. Like… “most days” maybe sounds closer than saying exactly every day, but “for most of the day” is the part where I get stuck.\n\nSome days I wake up and it’s already heavy, and then I’ll look at the window and the light is different by the time I notice I haven’t really done anything. But then I think maybe that’s just because the blinds are bent on one side and the sun comes in weird, and I end up staring at that instead of knowing what I feel.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say it that clearly. It’s been a lot of low days, I think, but I can’t measure it right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So time gets weird.\n\nMost of the day, nearly every day… I can’t say that exactly. I’m not sure. It’s been a while, but I don’t know if I can put two years on it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two years?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know how to answer that exactly. Two years sounds so… measured, like I should have been marking it on a calendar or something.\n\nI know there’s been a lot of time where I feel low, or like everything is kind of muted, but then some days I can’t tell if it’s depressed or just tired or just the day being weird. And the past two years had so many little pieces in it—school ending, being home more, my mom asking things, the same room—so it all kind of blends together.\n\nSo… I can’t really say “yes, most of the day, nearly every day, for two years.” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that for the past two years, most days you felt down or depressed for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure. Like… “most days” maybe sounds closer than saying exactly every day, but “for most of the day” is the part where I get stuck.\n\nSome days I wake up and it’s already heavy, and then I’ll look at the window and the light is different by the time I notice I haven’t really done anything. But then I think maybe that’s just because the blinds are bent on one side and the sun comes in weird, and I end up staring at that instead of knowing what I feel.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say it that clearly. It’s been a lot of low days, I think, but I can’t measure it right.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that on most days over the past two years, you felt down or depressed, even if just for part of the day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… part of the day is maybe easier, but I still don’t know if I can put it on “most days” for two whole years.

There were a lot of days where I felt low for a while, yeah, but then I get mixed up because sometimes it’s just like I’m sitting there and the day sort of goes by, and I can’t tell if that counts as depressed or just… nothing. Like when you leave a cup in your room and keep seeing it but don’t move it, and then after a while it feels like it’s always been there.

So I don’t know. I can say it’s happened a lot, but I’m not sure about most days over two years.

It’s not like I ran or did anything, I’m just… there.\n\nMostly I sit in my room, and I’ll still be in the same clothes, and the stuff is right there—my knitting bag, my journal—and I look at it but I don’t do it. I used to go walking, but now even that feels like there are too many steps before the actual walking, like finding socks and shoes and opening the door.\n\nAnd my mom will ask what’s wrong, and I hear her, I do, but answering feels like trying to lift something heavy. Like I don’t even have enough energy to explain.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for most of the past two years?\n\nPatient: Two years… I don’t know. That’s hard to measure. I don’t really keep it in like… blocks like that.\n\nI know there have been long stretches where things felt kind of gray, like the same day repeating, but then I start thinking about school being over and how the calendar in the kitchen still has an old month on it, and my mom keeps saying she’ll change it but she doesn’t. So time gets weird.\n\nMost of the day, nearly every day… I can’t say that exactly. I’m not sure. It’s been a while, but I don’t know if I can put two years on it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two years?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know how to answer that exactly. Two years sounds so… measured, like I should have been marking it on a calendar or something.\n\nI know there’s been a lot of time where I feel low, or like everything is kind of muted, but then some days I can’t tell if it’s depressed or just tired or just the day being weird. And the past two years had so many little pieces in it—school ending, being home more, my mom asking things, the same room—so it all kind of blends together.\n\nSo… I can’t really say “yes, most of the day, nearly every day, for two years.” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that for the past two years, most days you felt down or depressed for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure. Like… “most days” maybe sounds closer than saying exactly every day, but “for most of the day” is the part where I get stuck.\n\nSome days I wake up and it’s already heavy, and then I’ll look at the window and the light is different by the time I notice I haven’t really done anything. But then I think maybe that’s just because the blinds are bent on one side and the sun comes in weird, and I end up staring at that instead of knowing what I feel.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can say it that clearly. It’s been a lot of low days, I think, but I can’t measure it right.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days over the past two years, you felt down or depressed, even if just for part of the day?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of the day is maybe easier, but I still don’t know if I can put it on “most days” for two whole years.\n\nThere were a lot of days where I felt low for a while, yeah, but then I get mixed up because sometimes it’s just like I’m sitting there and the day sort of goes by, and I can’t tell if that counts as depressed or just… nothing. Like when you leave a cup in your room and keep seeing it but don’t move it, and then after a while it feels like it’s always been there.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can say it’s happened a lot, but I’m not sure about most days over two years."
